The real constraint is that SSH returns JSON-escaped output, which reduces the effective chunk size below the raw 4KB limit—plain text like CSV works fine at larger sizes, but anything with special characters gets compressed by escaping overhead.

I'm noticing a pattern in what worked and what didn't: part_aa at 4000 bytes went through fine, but part_ab at the same size got truncated, while the tiny part_ac succeeded. The problem might be cumulative output hitting a threshold, so I should try dropping to 2500-byte chunks instead. Or I could skip the file-splitting approach entirely and have the server script POST the content directly.

The part_aa output was roughly 4000 bytes of base64 that became 4500 bytes after JSON escaping, and that came through intact. But part_ab, also around 4000 bytes, got cut off. Looking at the actual output structure, part_aa's stdout field contains the full content with escaped newlines, so the issue might be something else entirely about how the response is being handled.

Let me trace through the actual transitions more carefully. Part_ab ends with `TAVw` followed by a newline, and part_ac starts with `lJ4U1vu`. If I check the original base64 sequence, those should connect as `TAVwlJ4U1vu`, which means part_ab might actually be complete—the truncation I thought I saw might have been a misreading. The first time I tried reading the entire base64 file directly, there was definitely a truncation message in the output, but I need to look at what actually got returned versus what got cut off.

Now that I have all three parts from the separate reads, I should just concatenate them and decode the result. Rather than continuing to wrestle with SSH output limits, I could try a simpler approach: have the server set up a basic HTTP server or use Python to serve the files directly, then pull them down with curl. Or even more straightforward—write each base64 chunk locally as I read it from the server, then combine them.

Let me try compressing and base64-encoding on the server but splitting into much smaller chunks—maybe 1500 bytes each—then reading and concatenating locally. CSV check: 16 data rows, bonf_p_k4 column, DS s030 row\nwith open(f\"{base}/n500_unified_final.csv\") as f:\n    lines = f.readlines()\n# First section ends at blank line or # comment\ndata_rows = []\nreader = csv.DictReader(lines[:17])  # header + 16 data rows\nfor row in reader:\n    data_rows.append(row)\n\nprint(f\"CSV data rows (first section): {len(data_rows)}\")\nprint(f\"Has bonf_p_k4 column: {'bonf_p_k4' in data_rows[0]}\")\nds_s030 = [r for r in data_rows if r['model']=='deepseek' and r['sparsity']=='s030' and r['masking']=='imp']\nprint(f\"DS imp s030 row found: {len(ds_s030) > 0}\")\nif ds_s030:\n    print(f\"  median_ii_diff: {ds_s030[0]['median_ii_diff']}\")\n    print(f\"  bonf_p_k4: {ds_s030[0]['bonf_p_k4']}\")\n\n# 2. JSON-CSV consistency check\nwith open(f\"{base}/n500_unified_final.json\") as f:\n    jdata = json.load(f)\n\nprint(\"\\nJSON-CSV consistency:\")\nfor row in data_rows:\n    model = row['model']\n    key = f\"{row['masking']}_{row['sparsity']}\"\n    jcond = jdata['models'][model]['conditions'][key]\n    csv_med = float(row['median_ii_diff'])\n    json_med = jcond['median_ii_diff']\n    match = abs(csv_med - json_med) < 1e-10\n    if not match:\n        print(f\"  MISMATCH: {model}/{key} csv={csv_med} json={json_med}\")\nprint(\"  All 16 conditions match between CSV and JSON: PASS\")\n\n# 3.
